| **[VibeVideoBench:Evaluating Multimodal Agents on Code-Driven Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35864v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion video models produce striking clips, but they cannot reliably render an exact formula, a correct chart, a ruleconsistent game or narration synchronized to on-screen events. A different route is emerging: multimodal agents that write code to render every frame and synthesize every sound. We introduce VibeVideoBench, a benchmark for evaluating agents on code-driven video generation. Its 239 self-contained tasks in English and Chinese span 12 categories, from data stories and music-driven pieces to classroom lessons and paper explainers; each asks an agent, running in its native harness inside a sandbox, to deliver a finished MP4 with sound within one hour. Evaluation is hierarchical and evidence-based: program checks of the delivery spec, about 13k task-specific rubric checks traced to the requirements they enforce, and five quality dimensions anchored per task, judged by three VLM judge panels and validated with blind human preferences. Across nine frontier agent systems and 2,151 scored rollouts, the benchmark is far from solved: GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5.5 lead clearly, yet even the best system fails a core requirement on about a quarter of tasks. Whether a video arrives at all matters as much as its quality, and three quarters of the lost videos are final renders started too late, run too slowly or never started. The judges agree with each other and with human preferences on system rankings, but only moderately on individual videos.</p></details> |  |
| **[Work Agents: Systems, Capabilities, and Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35845v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As foundation models evolve from information generation to digital work execution, agents are increasingly capable of completing complex tasks across tools, applications, and workflows. However, existing research lacks a unified perspective on how agents reliably accomplish delegated work. To address this gap, we present a work-centric, system-level perspective that views work agents as systems combining foundation models and runtime harnesses to execute tasks in stateful workspaces and deliver verifiable outcomes. We introduce two complementary dimensions, work persistence and work openness, to characterize working conditions, and classify digital work into five forms according to task scope and organization. Building on this framework, we identify five core capability families required for reliable work execution and examine approaches to model optimization, harness optimization, and their joint optimization. We further analyze existing evaluation methods and metrics from the perspective of work outcomes. Finally, we discuss future research directions, including sustained work delegation, adaptation to open environments, and the interaction between work execution and capability improvement. This paper provides a unified framework for understanding, optimizing, and evaluating work agents, supporting the development of systems capable of delivering reliable, verifiable, and practically valuable work outcomes.</p></details> |  |
| **[ACARE: Agent-Context-Aware Reranker for Iterative Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35840v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In agentic search, a document can match an agent-generated sub-query without helping answer the user's original question. Conventional rerankers score documents against the sub-query alone, overlooking the broader task and the agent's reasoning. We introduce the Agent-Context-Aware Reranker (ACARe), a compact 4B LLM trained to rerank documents using this context when available. Across multiple benchmarks and agent families, ACARe improves retrieval quality over its base reranker while reducing search effort. These benefits extend to answer generation: ACARe improves accuracy and reduces searches over no reranking, while matching or exceeding the base reranker's mean accuracy with more precise evidence. It also helps smaller agents narrow the retrieval-quality gap to larger agents, showing how better retrieval tools can make smaller agents more effective. The model, training data, and code will be released soon.</p></details> |  |
| **[Profiling a Frontier Agent with EBench: From Direct Control to Harness with Policy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35832v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Frontier multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can control robots without robot-specific training, but how their capabilities compare and interact with post-trained vision-language-action (VLA) and world action model (WAM) policies across manipulation demands remains to be thoroughly evaluated. In this report, we evaluate GPT-6-Astra on EBench as the core of an agent that controls a robot through an agent harness under the same visual and proprioceptive observation protocol as post-trained policies. We study two agent configurations: direct control, in which the agent generates waypoint commands directly, and harness with policy, in which it can also invoke a post-trained VLA or WAM policy for action execution. Under direct control, the agent achieves competitive overall performance relative to post-trained policies. It leads on short-horizon tasks and exhibits the least performance variation across generalization dimensions, whereas it underperforms post-trained policies on long-horizon tasks, tabletop manipulation, and high-precision contact-rich tasks. In the harness with policy, invoking a post-trained VLA or WAM combines the complementary strengths of both components, raising overall task success to the level of the strongest compared post-trained policies while substantially reducing token cost. However, performance remains below the per-task upper bound of the standalone components due to suboptimal policy invocation, fixed-instruction execution without subgoals, and grasp disruption when the agent resumes control. Overall, our study characterizes the capability boundary of a frontier MLLM agent and suggests directions for improving future agent harness design. Project page: https://internrobotics.github.io/EBench-Astra-Tech-Report/</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://internrobotics.github.io/EBench-Astra-Tech-Report/</p></details> |
| **[Back to Basics: A Skill-Centric Approach to Generalist Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35823v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Browser agents do not just need more practice. They need to practice the right things. We introduce MiniWeb, a realistic miniature of the web comprising 65 interconnected websites across 15 domains, where agents navigate, search, and transact as people do. Its 369 human-annotated tasks cover everyday workflows and label 37 reusable interaction primitives grouped into 7 skill families, allowing us to diagnose which skills fail rather than only whether a task succeeds. Benchmarking ten open-weight agents, we find that drag & gesture is consistently the weakest family, with no agent solving more than 24% of its instances. To address this gap, we introduce MiniWebGen, a framework that autonomously generates verified, skill-centric training data from MiniWeb. Fine-tuning Qwen3.5-4B on just 3.1K verified trajectories improves single-primitive success by 24.9pp across 13 unseen websites, with gains across every skill family. However, these trajectories teach short-horizon interactions, which do not directly translate to full-task success. We build on this result with MiniWebAgent, which pairs the trained executor with a planner that decomposes full tasks into the skill-level steps it learned to perform. This approach improves task success by 19.4pp points on unseen MiniWeb sites and transfers to external benchmarks, with gains of 9.3pp on WebArena-Lite, 16.1pp on Online-Mind2Web, and 20.0pp on WebVoyager, without training on their trajectories. We provide a simple recipe for compact browser agents: practice reusable skills directly, then compose them to solve unfamiliar tasks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Pretraining Knows What, Simulation Teaches How: Compositional Generalization in a Sim-Trained Robot Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35817v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A robot policy needs to understand what a task is asking for in a scene, and how to move the robot to accomplish that task. To achieve these two skills, we introduce Zero-real Agent Policy (ZAP), a recipe that teaches a small off-the-shelf vision-language model to control a robot via a simple tool harness. We use a pretrained 27B parameter model to bootstrap world understanding and reasoning, followed by supervised fine-tuning and GRPO on simple simulated tasks to teach it how to move the robot. Without any real-world robot data during training, ZAP reaches 96.7% success on real-world tasks, matching GPT-6 Astra in our harness and outperforming the MolmoAct2 VLA (33.3%), while running 3x faster and 17x cheaper than GPT-6 Astra. We demonstrate that ZAP also generalizes to completely new objects, goals, strategies, and language by seating a test tube in a rack (and choosing a different hole in the rack when prompted), handing a test tube between arms mid-air, drawing an X on a tic-tac-toe board, and playing chess. These goals and types of manipulation don't appear in our training data. Furthermore, ZAP also transfers to an unseen embodiment. On a single-arm Franka, ZAP reaches 55.9% on RoboLab-120, 4.9 percentage points ahead of the leading VLA/WAM baseline.</p></details> |  |
| **[FOCUS: Active Critic Learning for Long-Horizon Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35801v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sparse outcome rewards make it difficult for long-horizon language-model agents to assign credit to earlier actions. To mitigate these issues, we introduce FOCUS, an RL method that uses selective branching to help the critic network learn its value estimator. The rollout controller facilitates active learning for the critic by sampling continuations from states that are expected to most reduce the critic's estimation error. These refined value estimates then score the segment between consecutive states with a TD($λ$) advantage, while the sampled evidence supplies training targets for the critic. FOCUS supports long-horizon agentic reinforcement learning through a modular context-management interface that decouples context management from rollout allocation. Our experiments show that using compaction-induced segment boundaries for value estimation and credit assignment, FOCUS outperforms GRPO and VinePPO with matched per-step generation budgets on legal reasoning, deep search, and code localization benchmarks, improving held-out accuracy at Qwen3.5-9B scale by 7.2 points over the strongest baseline on BrowseComp-Plus (55.4 vs. 48.2) and by 4.1 points on DeonticBench (56.5 vs. 52.4).</p></details> | 32 pages, 5 figures |
| **[Quantifying Claim Smoothing by LLM Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35793v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deep research, or any use of large language models (LLMs) to read a large document collection and produce insights, requires synthesizing information from different sources. In collapsing diverse and potentially conflicting sources into a single narrative, the model discards the evidential "jaggedness" of its corpus and asserts conclusions more decisive than the citations can support. We introduce a claim level scoring pipeline, SmoothScore, to quantify this effect. Our pipeline extracts key conclusive claims from model generated reports, then finds evidence relevant to each claim and clusters that evidence along high level aspects. Finally, each claim is scored with respect to the evidence to quantify the degree of smoothing done by the agent. Across 7 systems and 4 datasets, we find that agents consistently produce stronger claims than warranted.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code ...</summary><p>Code and data present at: https://github.com/tiasa2/SmoothScore</p></details> |
| **[A Unified Arena for LLM Agents across Diverse Eurogame](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35773v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Interactive environments offer a practical way to evaluate large language model (LLM) agents, and competitive games extend this evaluation to decisions shaped by competing goals and other agents' choices. Yet existing evaluations confined to individual games or execution frameworks leave unclear which competitive advantages persist across settings. These games combine lasting strategic commitments, competition over shared resources, and multiple routes to victory. | **[Free to Look, Free to Reason: Simple LLM Harnesses Outdiagnose Hand-Crafted Root Cause Analysis Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35767v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Root cause analysis (RCA) of microservice failures has attracted many LLM agents whose investigation is hand-crafted, through standard operating procedures, fixed stages and role agents. A stronger model mainly improves reliability, a multi-turn harness mainly improves aptitude, and more reasoning effort changes accuracy by a few points at most while multiplying the time by up to seven. | **[Preference-Guided Cooperation in Multi-Objective Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35736v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-objective multi-agent reinforcement learning (MOMARL) addresses agent coordination and objective trade-offs simultaneously while optimising overall system performance. Existing work tends to treat multi-agent cooperation and multi-objective optimisation as largely independent components of a learning problem, which amounts to an exorbitant complexity except in trivial cases. We study here whether the structure provided by multiple objectives can itself be exploited to facilitate cooperation, rather than being treated as an additional optimisation challenge. We formulate MOMARL at both the team and individual levels and introduce objective-wise counterfactual credit assignment to evaluate each agent's contribution across the team objectives. By incorporating meta-gradient information, MIRL enables agents to learn how to prioritise objectives individually while collectively optimising the system-level objective, allowing the multi-objective structure to directly guide cooperation. Experiments on a multi-objective multiple particle environment show that our methods improve Pareto-front quality over baselines, with the dynamic variant achieving the strongest performance.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 4 figures, 1 table (plus 3 figures, 3 tables, and 2 algorithms in the appendix)</p></details> |
| **[Automated Standardization of Legacy Biomedical Metadata Using an Ontology-Constrained LLM Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08552v5)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Descriptive scientific metadata in public repositories are often incomplete and inconsistent with community standards and ontologies, limiting data FAIRness. | **[MiRL: Error-Targeted Reinforcement Learning for Correcting Terminal Agent Misbehaviors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35697v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Error-targeted learning gives a legible way to improve a model: identify specific weaknesses of the model, train the model to fix these weaknesses, and check whether the weaknesses are fixed. | **[MicroAgent: Context-Augmented Multi-Agent Framework for Automatic Microservice Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29742v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The adoption of Microservice Architecture (MSA) has revolutionized software engineering by enhancing scalability, agility, and maintainability over traditional monolithic applications. | **[EvoRouter: Cost-Efficient Multi-Agent Routing via Insight-Guided On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35690v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Serving web queries at scale with multi-agent systems requires jointly configuring large language models, collaboration modes, agent counts, interaction rounds, and skills. EvoRouter achieves an average in-domain accuracy of 68.69%, outperforming the strongest routing baseline by 7.23 percentage points while reducing inference compute cost by 50.95%. | **[FinAutoRubric: Expert-Guided Automatic Rubric Generation for Evaluating Financial Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35744v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluating finance research agents requires rubrics that reflect expert standards and fix the values correct as of an information cutoff. | **[Evaluate the Stack, Not the Layer: Do Deterministic and LLM Gates for Agent Actions Fail Independently?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07359v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Runtime gates for agent tool calls are stacked on the assumption that their errors multiply. We read each stack as a number of multiplication-equivalent layers, n_mult, with its floor under perfect coupling. Under the STRICT miss definition (escalation to a human scored as not stopped), any two judges compose to about 1.2 to 1.4 layers (φ median +0.430, 6 of 6 pairs significant, floors 1.02 to 1.17). The rule layer plus one judge composes to 1.86 to 2.09 layers (φ median +0.014, 0 of 4 significant, floors 1.01 to 1.09). Under PRIMARY (escalation scored as caught) the bands are 1.21 to 1.57 and 1.80 to 2.13. Intervals separate on the pooled data, point estimates split on each corpus, and a third-vendor judge lands in the judge band. Solo accuracy does not predict what a layer adds: a cloud rule pack lowers the rule layer's solo miss rate by 20% and adds no new joint coverage. The difficulty share of judge coupling is not identifiable: 31.8% to 61.8% depending on the probe and the miss definition. One judge tier was served by an unrequested model version in 50 of 112 batches, concentrated on the external corpus.
